<meta>This page is the title page for the 1859 edition of the Book of Jubilees. It includes library cataloging marks and pencil notations indicating its place in the University of Toronto collection. The original text is primarily in Latin, which was the standard language for academic publications in the 19th century, with the title also provided in Ge'ez (Classical Ethiopic).</meta>

->( The Book of Division <note>original Ge'ez: *Matshafa Kufale*. This is the traditional Ethiopian name for the Book of Jubilees, referring to how the book divides history into jubilee periods</note> )<-

->OR<-

# BOOK OF JUBILEES

->WHICH IS THE SAME<-

## CALLED BY THE GREEKS *THE LITTLE GENESIS* <note>original Greek: *He lepte Genesis*. "Little" or "Fine" Genesis refers to the book's tendency to provide more minute, detailed accounts of the stories found in the biblical Book of Genesis</note>

->THE GREEK VERSION BEING LOST<-

->NOW PRESERVED ONLY IN THE GE’EZ LANGUAGE<- <note>Ge'ez is the ancient liturgical and literary language of Ethiopia, similar to how Latin was used in Europe</note>

->RECENTLY BROUGHT FROM ABYSSINIA <note>A historical name for Ethiopia</note> TO EUROPE.<-

---

### IN ETHIOPIC

->BASED ON THE RELIABILITY OF TWO MANUSCRIPT BOOKS<-

->FIRST EDITED BY<-

## **DR. AUGUST DILLMANN**

->PROFESSOR AT KIEL.<-

---

->1859 <insert>(1859)</insert><-

->AT THE EXPENSE OF THE EDITOR. PRINTED BY F. WILHELM KAESTNER AT GÖTTINGEN.<-

->FOR SALE<-

->**IN KIEL** &emsp; &emsp; **IN LONDON**<-

->BY C. G. L. VAN MAACK. &emsp; &emsp; BY WILLIAMS AND NORGATE.<-

<summary>This is the title page for the first modern scholarly edition of the Book of Jubilees, edited by August Dillmann in 1859. It highlights that while the original Greek text was lost, the work survived in the Ge'ez language of Ethiopia.</summary>

<keywords>Book of Jubilees, August Dillmann, Ge'ez, Little Genesis, Ethiopia, Biblical scholarship, 19th century, manuscript studies</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page is the Latin preface to the 1859 critical edition of the Ethiopic "Book of Jubilees" (Kufâlê), likely written by the scholar August Dillmann. It details the manuscript sources used to reconstruct the text.</meta>

About to hand over to you, kind reader, a printed book of ancient **Judaism**, most worthy of remembrance and rescued from the dark fate of oblivion, I will briefly explain both the resources and the reasoning behind my edition.

About a decade ago, when I published a German translation of the **Book of Jubilees** based on the **Tübingen** manuscript, I publicly admitted that an edition of the **Ethiopic** book itself could hardly be prepared until another copy brought from **Abyssinia** <note>original: "Abyssinia," the historical name for Ethiopia</note> came into my hands. The longing for a new copy that I had felt since that time was fulfilled sooner than I expected. **Antoine d’Abbadie**—whom I had heard from the renowned scholar **H. Ewald** possessed a hidden copy of **Jubilees** in his vast collection of **Ethiopic** manuscripts—most generously permitted me to use it for my work, showing the exceptional kindness for which he is distinguished. Having examined it, and realizing that a sufficiently reliable edition of **Jubilees** could be compiled from these two books, I did not wish to delay the completion of the work any longer.

There are, therefore, two manuscripts that I used. The first is the **Tübingen** manuscript, kept in the public university library (**M. a. IX. 4**), which I will designate hereafter with the symbol **T**. It is made of paper, in a quarto size, consisting of **80** leaves. It was transcribed somewhat carelessly in **Abyssinia** by the assistant of the Reverend **J. L. Krapff**, and it contains only the book of **Kufâlê** <note>The Ethiopic title of the Book of Jubilees, meaning "The Book of Division"</note>. This manuscript was sent here by the most distinguished **R. Roth**, my very dear friend, with permission most generously granted by the governing board of the library. The other, marked by me with the symbol **A**, is the d’Abbadie manuscript (designated as number **117** [CXVII]) ¹), made of parchment, in a very large quarto format,

---

1) One may consult the catalog of the d’Abbadie library, published this year: *Annotated Catalog of Ethiopic Manuscripts belonging to Antoine d'Abbadie*. Paris, Imperial Printing House, **1859** [MDCCCLIX], pages 132 and 133.

<summary>In this preface, the editor explains how he acquired two primary Ethiopic manuscripts—one from Tübingen and one from the collector Antoine d’Abbadie—to create the first reliable printed edition of the Book of Jubilees.</summary>
<keywords>Book of Jubilees, Ethiopic manuscripts, Antoine d’Abbadie, August Dillmann, Kufâlê, Tübingen University, biblical scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the Latin preface to August Dillmann's 1859 edition of the Ethiopic Book of Jubilees. The previous page introduced the first manuscript (T), a paper copy from Tübingen. This page describes the second, more significant manuscript (A) obtained from Antoine d'Abbadie.</meta>

**VI**

It consists of **163** leaves, with the pages divided into three columns, elegantly written by a single hand. **Antoine d'Abbadie** purchased it from a man of the **Falasha** people <note>The Beta Israel community of Ethiopia</note>. It appears to be no older than the current century <note>the 19th century, relative to the 1859 publication date</note>. It contains the **Octateuch** <note>The first eight books of the Old Testament</note> and, in the final **34** leaves, the book of **Kufâlê** <note>The Book of Jubilees</note>. However, while it offers a fairly good version of the **Octateuch**—one that matches the manuscript marked **C** in my edition of that work—the copy of **Jubilees** suffers from a vast number of errors and mistakes; it is hardly better than the **Tübingen** manuscript.

It is therefore no surprise that throughout the book of **Kufâlê**, a different hand has either written corrections above the lines and in the margin <margin>1)</margin>, or has used thick marks—perhaps "obelisks" <note>original: "obelis?"—editorial marks used since antiquity to indicate suspected or corrupt text</note> or rather charcoal "clubs" rubbed into the margin—to highlight the corrupt readings found there. Among the corrections added by this editor, some agree with the readings in the **Tübingen** manuscript (**T**), while others differ from both my primary manuscript (**A**) and **T**. Since it is not always clear whether these corrections are based on the authority of another book or simply the editor’s own guesses, I have selected whatever seemed useful and recorded them in the annotations under the symbol **E** <note>original: "siglo E," meaning the siglum or abbreviation used in the critical apparatus</note>.

Given the nature of manuscripts **A** and **T**, comparing the two inevitably resulted in a vast number of differing readings. Most of these variations admittedly concern only rules of spelling and grammar, but many also affect the meaning of the text. Far from suggesting that these manuscripts represent two different versions of the book, the variations can be explained either by the carelessness of scribes and accidental errors, or by the natural evolution of the **Ge’ez** <note>The ancient Ethiopic liturgical language</note> language across different regions and eras. Therefore, it was not my task as editor simply to transcribe one manuscript and note the variations of the other in the margin. Instead, it seemed better suited to my goal to select the superior reading from either manuscript, attempting to restore the original wording of the book as far as my limited resources allowed...

---

<margin>1) Furthermore, these corrections are only added consistently at the beginning of the book; they gradually become rarer and eventually cease.</margin>

<summary>Dillmann describes the second manuscript (A), a 19th-century text from the Falasha community, and explains his editorial method of selecting the best readings from both sources to reconstruct the original Ge'ez text.</summary>
<keywords>Antoine d'Abbadie, Falasha, Octateuch, Book of Jubilees, Ge'ez, manuscript variants, editorial methodology</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues August Dillmann's Latin preface to his 1859 critical edition of the Ethiopic Book of Jubilees (Kufâlê). Having described the physical manuscripts in the previous pages, he now explains his editorial methodology—specifically how he decided which "variant readings" (different versions of the same sentence) to include in his footnotes and when he felt it was necessary to correct the text himself.</meta>

->**VII**<-

...he might attempt to restore the text. But so that readers would not depend solely upon the judgment of a single editor, I have placed variant readings <note>different versions of the text found in different manuscripts</note> at the bottom of each page. From these, I have recorded all those that seemed to hold value for understanding the words in different ways. However, regarding those variants which pertain only to the rules of grammar or serve merely to prove the negligence of the scribes, I have added many but omitted many more <note>1)</note>: for it is a vain pursuit that brings boredom to both editor and reader to attempt to transcribe every "hallucination" of the Ethiopian <note>original: "Abyssinicorum"</note> scribes, such as they are today.

You have here, therefore, the text of the *Jubilees* just as it was possible to weave it together from the manuscript books. Although in many places I might have conjectured <note>2)</note> <note>Conjecture: an educated guess by an editor to fix a word that seems broken in all surviving copies</note> better readings than those the books provide, I have nevertheless introduced emendations into the body of the text or the notes only where it was possible to "heal" clearly corrupt or empty readings of the books by some slight change of vowels or consonants. I have explicitly stated what was my own contribution and what belonged to the manuscripts. Those things which I corrected silently pertain only to spelling <term>orthography|The correct way of writing/spelling words</term> and grammar <note>3)</note>: for in such matters, no authority should be attributed to books produced so late and written so carelessly. Other variations of this kind, which seemed tolerable, I have left untouched <note>4)</note>, as well as proper names, however much cor- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "corrupt"</note>

---

<note>1) For example, on page 48, Codex **T** usually shows **ጸጕር፡** <note>ṣagʷr; hair</note> instead of **ጶጕር፡** <note>p̣ogʷr</note>.</note>

<note>2) For example: on page 39 line 17, it seems one should read **ለሊሁ፡** <note>himself</note> instead of **ለወልዱ፡** <note>for his son</note>; on page 40 line 13 **አምሐሉ፡** <note>they swore</note> instead of **አምሐሊ፡** <note>one who causes to swear</note>; page 61 line 16 **ነፋስቲሁሙ፡** instead of **ነፋሰቲሁሙ፡** <note>their spirits/winds</note>; page 112 line 6 **ውሉደ፡** <note>children</note> instead of **ውሉዱ፡** <note>his children</note>; on page 74 line 6 **ትምክሕት፡** <note>pride/boasting</note> is contrary to the proper sense of the passage.</note>

<note>3) For example, for the word **ሂዋን፡**, which the manuscripts provide almost everywhere, and for **ሔዋን፡** <note>Eve</note>, and for **ልሳናት፡** <note>tongues/languages</note> which they occasionally provide, I have restored the standard forms **ሂዋን፡**, **ሔዋን፡**, and **ልሳናት፡**. Likewise, on page 22 line 8, I substituted the reading **ይትወለጠ፡** <note>be changed</note> for **ይትወለወ፡**, and on page 24 line 14, **ይትገን፡** <note>be subdued</note> for **ይንትጊ፡**.</note>

<note>4) For example: page 18 line 11, the Subjunctive instead of the Indicative **ይረስዩ፡** <note>they make/place</note>; page 33 line 4, the form **ደሞክሙ፡** instead of **ደምክሙ፡** <note>your blood</note>; line 6, **ለዘህለወ፡** instead of the simpler **ዘህለወ፡** <note>which was</note>; page 37 line 17, **መንገለ፡ ሀለባ፡** instead of the simpler **ሀለባ፡** <note>milk</note>; page 44 line 9, I set **ሀለባ፡** in the absolute state as **ሀለበ፡**; page 60 line 18, the form **ውውሉደ፡** is preferred over **ውሉደ፡** <note>children</note>; page 50 line 18, **ይሰልጡ፡** <note>they prevail</note> is a less-than-ideal form.</note>

<summary>In this page of the preface, August Dillmann details his editorial philosophy for the Book of Jubilees, explaining that he includes significant variant readings while filtering out minor scribal errors. He defends his use of "conjecture" to fix broken text and notes that he only silently corrects basic spelling and grammar due to the poor quality of the late-period manuscripts.</summary>

<keywords>August Dillmann, Book of Jubilees, Kufâlê, textual criticism, Ethiopic manuscripts, Ge'ez grammar, orthography, scribal errors, editorial method</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues August Dillmann’s Latin preface to his critical edition of the Ethiopic Book of Jubilees. On the previous page, Dillmann began explaining how he handled errors in the manuscripts. He concludes a thought here about how he dealt with "broken" grammar and Greek terms before detailing specific passages where he filled in missing text using different manuscripts (Codex T and Codex A) and where the text remains unfortunately damaged beyond repair.</meta>

->**VIII**<-

...rammar, and **Greek** nouns, which the translator <note>The original translator who translated the book from Greek into Ethiopic centuries ago</note> did not wish to explain; I have described them exactly as I found them. But I must add a warning for students: they should be careful not to try to innovate anything in matters of grammar based on the authority of such passages.

Now, regarding the actual matters narrated in this book, the text, which I was able to piece together from two manuscripts, still suffers from various difficulties and errors. It fortunately happened that several gaps <term>lacunae|Missing sections or "holes" in a manuscript</term> in manuscript **T** and the **German** version <note>Dillmann's own previous German translation</note> could be filled from manuscript **A**, such as on page **12** regarding **Eve’s** introduction into paradise; p. **23** lines **6** and **7**; p. **39** regarding the inheritance of **Lud**, son of **Shem**; p. **51** regarding the restoration of the **Hebrew** language; p. **67** regarding **Abraham’s** trials; other passages were successfully corrected, most notably page **18** line **8** concerning the years that **Enoch** spent with the angels. 

But there remain passages which, being corrupt or mutilated in both **T** and **A**, could not be "healed," such as: (to say nothing of the corrupted numbers of years, months, and days) p. **23** line **1**; p. **28** line **2**; p. **31** line **4** and following; p. **43** regarding the building of the tower; p. **54** line **19** where the account of the victory won by **Abraham** is missing; p. **74** line **15**; p. **104** line **16** concerning the flocks of **Jacob** and **Laban**; p. **107** line **15**. Other passages, such as p. **18** regarding **Enoch’s** sacrifice, even now remain very obscure or open to suspicion. I consider it sufficient to have touched upon these defects in passing; for the medicine required to cure them is still lacking. 

And although one must not despair that some of these corrupt passages might be corrected if other copies of the book are imported from **Abyssinia** <note>Ethiopia</note>, nevertheless, one must not overlook the fact that the damage caused to this work by the toll of centuries seems to have crept into all copies used by the **Ethiopians** <note>original: "Abyssini"</note>. For other biblical books, if any damage had been inflicted, the **Ethiopians** of a more recent age could easily draw supplements from **Greek**, **Arabic**, **Syriac**, and **Coptic** versions; but in the case of the Book of **Kufâlê** <note>The Ethiopic name for the Book of Jubilees</note>, as it had been lost among other nations, the opportunity for repairing such damage was missing.

The punctuation marks, with which I have separated individual sentences and parts of sentences from one another, rest not on the authority of the manuscripts but on my own judgment,

<summary>Dillmann explains how he used two primary manuscripts to fill gaps in the text of Jubilees but admits that some passages remain "corrupt" because the book was lost in all languages except Ethiopic, preventing cross-referencing with other traditions.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript gaps, Codex T, Codex A, Kufâlê, Enoch's sacrifice, Abyssinia, text restoration, biblical scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues August Dillmann’s Latin preface to his critical edition of the Ethiopic Book of Jubilees. On the previous page, Dillmann discussed the difficulties of correcting the manuscript text and listed specific passages where Manuscript A helped fill gaps left by Manuscript T.</meta>

->**IX**<-

As I have done in my editions of other **Ethiopic** works, I wish to remind you here as well, kind reader, of one point. No importance should be placed on how punctuation is marked in manuscripts of a more recent age; for all who are experienced in **Ethiopic** manuscripts know well how absurd and truly contradictory the punctuation of the scribes tends to be.

Furthermore, the division of the book into fifty chapters, which I have noted in the margin, was made by me and transferred here from the **German** version in which I first proposed it. In manuscript **T**, no division of the text occurs at all, nor are the beginnings of individual parts indicated, except very rarely by small spaces left between sequences of words. In manuscript **A**, however, the scribe has written <term>Me’raf|Ethiopic: ምዕራፍ, meaning "chapter" or "section"</term> in red ink in many places, but he neglected to record the chapter numbers. Additionally, no consistent logic is apparent in this division; for example, between chapters **V** and **XXI** of our edition, not a single chapter is marked in that manuscript. Nevertheless, I did not want you to be unaware of at least those chapter beginnings that he did mark. Therefore, wherever his chapters do not align with mine, I have made their beginnings visible by inserting spaces in the text¹); the places where his chapters coincide with the start of mine are these: Chapters **2, 3, 5, 22, 24, 25, 26, 34, 36, 37, 39, 40, 45, 49.**

Rarer words unknown to **Hiob Ludolf** <note>original: "J. Ludolfo"; Ludolf was a 17th-century scholar regarded as the founder of Ethiopic studies in Europe</note> which occur in **Jubilees**, I will explain in my **Ethiopic** lexicon, which I intend to send to the press next year. Some of these can be easily understood from the context and the sense of the sentences, or from my **German** translation; certain others are very obscure and, as far as I know, are found nowhere except in this book.

It is already evident that my **German** translation must be corrected and improved in many places based on this edition of the **Ethiopic** work. Indeed, to say nothing of those passages where **T** provides inferior readings, even some of those readings which have now been confirmed by manuscript **A** were [previously translated incorrectly] due to their corruption...

***

1) Also on page 5, line 3, at the words <term>ze-we’etu wa-zentu|Ethiopic: ዘውእቱ፡ ወዘንቱ፡, meaning "this and that"</term>, the scribe began a new <term>Me’raf</term>, but since this was done through an obvious error, I have omitted marking it in my edition.

<summary>Dillmann explains his editorial choices regarding punctuation and chapter divisions, noting that he created the 50-chapter structure himself. He also mentions that rare vocabulary found in Jubilees will be included in his upcoming Ethiopic dictionary.</summary>
<keywords>punctuation, Hiob Ludolf, manuscript T, manuscript A, chapter divisions, Ethiopic lexicon, Book of Jubilees, translation corrections</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page concludes August Dillmann’s Latin preface to his critical edition of the Ethiopic Book of Jubilees. On the previous page, he discussed his methodology for dividing chapters and his reliance on manuscripts T and A, specifically noting his intent to explain rare Ethiopic terms.</meta>

->**X**<-

Since [the readings] of the manuscript seemed suspect to me, I have interpreted the **Ethiopic** words in various places by conjecture instead ¹). Those who have considered for themselves the difficulties of translating a book from a single manuscript of inferior quality will easily excuse errors of this kind. I currently have no leisure to work out a new interpretation of **Jubilees**; whether I shall obtain such leisure hereafter, I truly do not know; **God Best and Greatest** <note>original: "Deus O. M.", or *Deus Optimus Maximus*</note> alone knows. Regarding the age, the author, the subject matter, and the purpose of **Jubilees**, I would wish to refer you to my commentary, printed together with the **German** version; you may also see the titles of all the commentaries written on this book up to this point, listed below in footnote ²). **Farewell and be well!**

---

1) For example, instead of *sagamata* (page 49 line 2) <note>original: ስገመት፡</note> I had once conjectured *rezmat* <note>original: ርዝመት፡, meaning "length"</note>, but *sagamata* has now been confirmed by manuscript A (it means: *to be mute, to become silent*). Likewise, on page 50 line 20, I had rashly read *wadabē* <note>original: ወደቤ፡</note> instead of *wa’abē* <note>original: ወአቤ፡</note>.
2) The German version of Jubilees, along with a commentary on the age, author, subject matter, and purpose of the book, was published by me in the **Yearbooks of Biblical Science by H. Ewald** <note>original: "Jahrbücher der biblischen Wissenschaft von H. Ewald"</note>, **Yearbook II 1849, pages 230–256 and Yearbook III 1850–51, pages 1–96.** Subsequently, the following have discussed this book: **A. Jellinek** in **The House of Study** <note>original: "Bet ha-Midrasch"</note> part III, Leipzig 1855, pages IX–XIV; **Z. Frankel** in the **Monthly Journal for the History and Science of Judaism** <note>original: "Monatsschrift für Geschichte und Wissenschaft des Judenthums"</note> 1856, pages 311–316 and 380–400; **B. Beer** in **The Book of Jubilees and its Relationship to the Midrashim** <note>original: "das Buch der Jubiläen und sein Verhältniss zu den Midraschim"</note>, Leipzig 1856, and in **Another Word on the Book of Jubilees** <note>original: "Noch ein Wort über das Buch der Jubiläen"</note>, Leipzig 1857; **M. J. Krüger** in the **Journal of the German Oriental Society** <note>original: "Zeitschrift der deutschen morgenländischen Gesellschaft"</note>, Volume XII, 1858, pages 279–299. Furthermore, one may consult the **Journal of the German Oriental Society**, Volume XI, 1857, pages 161–163, and the **Yearbook of Biblical Science by H. Ewald** VIII, 1856, pages 184–185 and 291.

**Written at Kiel.** <note>original: "Kiliae"</note>

**The Editor.**

<summary>In the conclusion of his preface, Dillmann acknowledges the potential for errors due to poor manuscript quality and refers readers to his previous German scholarship and other contemporary studies on the Book of Jubilees. He expresses uncertainty about his future ability to produce a more definitive translation, leaving the matter to divine providence.</summary>
<keywords>August Dillmann, Book of Jubilees, manuscript criticism, Ethiopic scholarship, 19th-century biblical studies, H. Ewald, Kiel, bibliography</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page marks the transition from August Dillmann’s Latin preface to the actual Ethiopic text of the Book of Jubilees. Having concluded his introductory remarks on methodology and source manuscripts (specifically Manuscript A from the British Museum and Manuscript T from Tübingen), he now presents the opening of the Ge’ez version of the text, often called "Kufale" or "The Division."</meta>

# The Book of the Division of Days

This is the account of the **division** <term>Kufale: The Ethiopic title of the book, meaning "Division" or "Distribution," referring to how history is partitioned into specific time periods</term> of the days of the law and the testimony <gloss>1</gloss>, for the events of the years, for their weeks <gloss>2</gloss>, and for their **Jubilees** <term>Jubilees: Periods of 49 years, consisting of seven "weeks" of seven years each</term> throughout all the years of the world, just as **the Lord** <gloss>3</gloss> spoke to Moses on Mount Sinai when he went up to receive the tablets of stone containing the law and the commandments, by the word of the Lord, as He said to him: "Come up to the top of the mountain" <gloss>4</gloss>.

And it happened in the first year of the exodus of the children of Israel from Egypt, in the third month, on the sixteenth day of that month, that the Lord spoke to Moses, saying: "Come up to Me **here** <gloss>5</gloss> on the mountain, and I will give you the two tablets of stone containing the law and the commandments which I have begun to write, so that you may teach them." And Moses went up to the mountain of God, and the glory of the Lord dwelt upon Mount Sinai, and a cloud overshadowed it for six days. And the Lord called to Moses on the seventh day from the midst of the cloud; and the appearance of the glory of the Lord was like a burning fire on the **top of the mountain** <gloss>6</gloss>. And Moses was **there** <gloss>7</gloss> on the mountain for forty days and forty nights, and the Lord taught him the account of what was from the beginning.

---

<gloss>1</gloss> Manuscript A reads: "days of the law and for the testimony"; Manuscript E reads: "of the days of the law and testimony."
<gloss>2</gloss> Manuscript A reads: "and for their weeks."
<gloss>3</gloss> This word is omitted in Manuscript T.
<gloss>4</gloss> Manuscript T displays this entire sentence below, on line 15, after the words "top of the mountain."
<gloss>5</gloss> Omitted in Manuscript A.
<gloss>6</gloss> Manuscript T reads: "Mount Sinai."
<gloss>7</gloss> Omitted in Manuscript T.

<summary>This page contains the prologue and opening verses of the Book of Jubilees, detailing Moses ascending Mount Sinai to receive the law and a revelation of history divided into sabbatical cycles.</summary>
<keywords>Jubilees, Kufale, Moses, Mount Sinai, Law, Tablets of Stone, Chronology, Exodus, Ge'ez literature</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the prologue of the Book of Jubilees, moving into the specific instructions given to Moses on Mount Sinai. The text transitions from the narrative setting into the divine speech regarding the future of Israel and the necessity of recording the law to witness against their future apostasy. I have maintained continuity with the previous page's numbering and themes of the "Division of Days."</meta>

And he brought the word <gloss>1</gloss> of the **division** of the days, and for the law and for the testimony <gloss>2</gloss>.
And He said: "Set your heart to every word that I tell you on this mountain <gloss>3</gloss>, and write it in a book so that their generations may see how they did not return <gloss>4</gloss> <note>original: "ኢያገበው" - a scribal variation suggesting failing to turn back or repent</note> from all the evil which they committed, leading to error and the overturning <gloss>5</gloss> of the ordinance that I establish—and which I have spoken to you this day—for their generations on **Mount Sinai**. 

And it shall happen <gloss>6</gloss> and be told <gloss>7</gloss> in this way: when all the plagues that I have spoken <gloss>8</gloss> come upon them, they will know that I am more righteous than they <gloss>9</gloss> in all their judgments and in all their deeds <gloss>1</gloss><gloss>0</gloss>, and they will know that I have truly been with them. 

And as for you, write for yourself all <gloss>1</gloss><gloss>1</gloss> the words which I make known to you this day; for I know their rebelliousness and their **stiff-neckedness** <note>A common biblical metaphor for stubbornness or pride</note>, even before I bring them into the land which I swore to their fathers <gloss>1</gloss><gloss>2</gloss>, to **Abraham**, to **Isaac**, and to **Jacob**, saying: 'To your offspring I will give this <gloss>1</gloss><gloss>3</gloss> land, which flows with milk and honey, and they shall eat and be satisfied' <gloss>1</gloss><gloss>4</gloss>. 

Then they will turn toward strange gods who cannot save them from all their tribulations, and this testimony will be heard against them <gloss>1</gloss><gloss>5</gloss> as a witness <gloss>1</gloss><gloss>6</gloss>. For they will forget every commandment of mine which I command them, and they will walk after the Gentiles and after their uncleanness and after their shame. They will serve their gods, and these will become to them a cause of stumbling, of tribulation, of pain, and a snare. And many shall be destroyed, and they shall be taken captive, and they shall fall into the hand of the enemy, because they have abandoned my ordinance and my commandments."

And **Moses** fell on his face.

...and earth, and all that He created in the seven days. <note>variant in Codex A: "Creator of the heavens and the earth"</note> And the Lord <term>Egzi'abher: the standard Ethiopic name for God, often meaning 'Lord of the Universe'</term> gave a holy festival day to all His creatures; <note>variant in Codex A: "And He gave to us"</note> because of this, He commanded concerning it that whoever does any work on it shall die; <note>variant in Codex T: "for it"</note> and whoever defiles it shall surely die. <note>variant in Codex A: "dying, he shall die"</note>

It then transitions into the "second week" of the creation narrative, focusing on Adam naming the animals and the subsequent creation of Eve from Adam's rib.</meta>

...all, and He sanctified¹ all the peoples and nations so that they might observe it, except for **Israel** <note>The descendants of Jacob, chosen as God’s special people</note> alone. To him alone² did He give³ it so that he might eat and drink and observe it upon the earth. And the Creator of all things, who created this day, blessed it for a blessing, for holiness, and for glory above all days. This law and testimony was given to the children of Israel as an everlasting law for their generations.

And on the sixth day of the second **Sabbath** <note>In this context, "Sabbath" refers to the second week of the world's existence</note>, **God** <term>Egzi'abher: the standard Ethiopic name for God, often meaning 'Lord of the Universe'</term> brought⁴ by His word to him [Adam] all the wild animals, all the livestock⁵, all the birds, all that moves upon the earth, and all that moves in⁶ the water, each according to their kind and according to their likeness⁷. 

[He brought] the wild animals and livestock on the first day; the birds on the second day; all that moves on the earth on the third day; and all that moves in the water on the fourth day. And **Adam** named them all with their respective names, and whatever he called them, that became their name. 

And during those⁸ days, Adam saw all these—male and female—of every kind that is on the earth, but he himself was alone, and he found no⁹ companion to help him who was like himself. 

And **God**¹⁰ said to us: "It is not good for the man to be alone; let us make for him a helper like himself." 

And **God**¹¹ our Lord cast a deep sleep upon him, and he slept. And He took for the woman one bone from among his bones—this was the **Phanorita** <term>Phanorita: a specific term used in the Ethiopic Book of Jubilees, likely derived from a Greek word for "visible," referring to a specific rib</term> rib from among his bones—and He fashioned flesh in its place and built the woman¹².

<meta>This page continues the creation narrative from page 11, specifically the moment Adam wakes to find Eve has been created from his rib. It provides the theological justification for the purification laws found later in the Torah (Leviticus 12), linking the timing of Adam and Eve's entry into Eden to the periods of uncleanness after childbirth.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>

And He awakened Adam from his sleep, and he woke up. He arose on the sixth day and came to her <note>original: "ኀቤሃ" (to her). Manuscript T suggests "to him," implying God brought her to Adam.</note> and recognized her. And he said to her: "This is **now** bone of my bone and flesh of my flesh; she shall be called my wife <note>The Ge'ez "be'esit" (wife/woman) is the feminine form of "be'esi" (husband/man), mirroring the Hebrew wordplay of Ish and Ishshah.</note>, because from her husband she **was taken and she arose.**" For this reason, man and woman are one; and for this reason, a man **shall leave** his father and mother and join with his wife, and they shall become one flesh. 

And in the first week, Adam was created, and also the rib—his wife. In the **second** week, He showed her to him. For this reason, a commandment was given to keep <term>impurity: a state of ritual separation (Hebrew: 'niddah')</term> for seven days for a male, and for a female, another seven days.

And after forty days were completed for Adam in the land where he was created, they brought him into this **Eden** to work it and to keep it. And as for his wife, they brought her in on the 80th day, and after this, she entered into this **Eden**. And because of this, a commandment was written on the **heavenly tablets** <note>A recurring concept in the Book of Jubilees, suggesting that earthly laws are copies of eternal laws inscribed in heaven.</note> regarding her who gives birth: if she bears a male, she shall remain in her impurity for seven days, just as the first seven days [of Adam], and for thirty-three days she shall remain in the blood of her purification. She shall not touch anything holy, nor shall she enter the **sanctuary** until she completes these days for a male. 

And for a female, [the law is] two weeks, just as the **two weeks** of the beginning in impurity, and sixty-six days she shall remain in the blood of her purification; all together they are eighty days. And when she completed those eighty days, they brought her into this **Eden**, for **it is** holier than all...

And at the end of the thirty-ninth jubilee, in the seventh week, in its seventh year, **Adam** died. And all his children buried him in the tomb of his creation. And he was the first to be buried in a tomb. And he suffered in pain for ninety years out of his nine hundred and thirty years. <margin>M.</margin> For nine hundred and thirty years are like **one day** in the testimony of the heavens. And because of this, it was written concerning the **Tree of Knowledge**: "In the day that you eat from it, you shall die." And because of this, he did not complete the years of that day <note>A "day" in God's time is 1,000 years; since Adam died at 930, he died within that single "divine day."</note>, for he died within it. 

At the end of this jubilee, **Cain** was killed after him in the [same] nine hundred and thirtieth year. His house fell upon him, and he died in the middle of his house, and he was killed by its stones. For with a stone he had killed **Abel**, and by a stone he was killed in righteous judgment.

Concerning what was ordained on the **Heavenly Tablets** <note>original: "ጽላት፡ ሰማይ" (Tsillata Samay). **5** And it happened when the children of men began to multiply upon the *face of the whole earth$^2$* and daughters were born to them, that the **Angels of God** $^3$ <note>These are the "Watchers," celestial beings who, according to this tradition, descended to earth.</note> saw them in a certain *year$^4$* of this jubilee; for they were beautiful to look upon. And they took for themselves wives from among all $^5$ those whom they chose, and they bore children for them, *and they$^6$* were **Giants** <note>original: "ረሀይት" (Rehayt). These are the legendary Nephilim, a race of giants born from the union of angels and humans.</note>. 

And lawlessness increased upon the earth, and all flesh corrupted its way, from humans to livestock, and to wild beasts, and to birds, and to everything that moves upon the earth; they all corrupted *their way$^7$* and *their order$^7$* and began to devour one another $^8$. And lawlessness increased upon the earth, and *the thoughts of the knowledge$^9$* of all the children of men were thus evil all their days. And **God** looked upon the earth, and behold, it was corrupted, and all flesh had corrupted its order, and He looked at all of them before His eyes, *all$^{10}$* who were upon the earth. And He said, "I *will destroy$^{11}$* humanity and all flesh upon the face of the earth which was created." But **Noah** alone found favor before the eyes of the Lord.

<vocab>ሕግ (law), ኖኅ (Noah), በዓላት (festivals), እስራኤል (Israel), ዓመት (year), ሰንበታት (Sabbaths/weeks), ታቦት (ark), ኪዳን (covenant)</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the origins of the quarterly days of remembrance based on key milestones of Noah’s Flood, such as the drying of the earth and the closing of the abyss. It establishes a 364-day solar calendar consisting of exactly fifty-two weeks, divided into four thirteen-week quarters.</summary>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the divine instructions given to Moses on Mount Sinai regarding the sacred calendar. The previous section established the four quarterly memorial days rooted in the life of Noah and the Great Flood. The text now transitions into a stern warning about maintaining the 364-day solar count to ensure the festivals remain in their proper seasons, contrasting this with the "errors" of the lunar calendar.</meta>

...as it was carved¹ and ordained upon the **heavenly tablets**, and there is no deviation from it. 

The year consists of **three hundred and sixty-four days** <note>original: "፫፻ወ፷፬" (364)</note> and a year². And you shall command the children of Israel to observe the years according to this count: <gloss>100</gloss> three hundred <gloss>60</gloss> sixty-four days in **four divisions** <note>These are the four seasons or quarters of 91 days each.</note>, and it shall be a complete year. They shall not corrupt³ its timing, neither the days nor the festivals, for everything will happen for them according to their testimony. They shall not skip a day⁴ nor ruin a festival. 

For if they neglect and do not act according to His command⁵, then all their seasons will be ruined, and the years will be disturbed from their order. Both the seasons and the years⁶ will be unsettled, and their regulations will be neglected. Then all the children of Israel will forget⁷ and will not find the correct path of the years; they will forget the **new moon** <note>original: "ሠርቀ" (sarqa), the beginning of the lunar month or a solar marker in this context.</note>, the Sabbath, and the festivals; they will err in the entire regulation of the years. 

For I know, and from this moment I declare it to you—and not from my own heart, for this is the book written before me and ordained on the **heavenly tablets**—regarding the division of days: they will forget the festivals of the covenant and walk in the festivals of the Gentiles, following after their error and their ignorance. 

There will be those who make **observations** <term>observations: "ሐዋጾ" (hawaso), a technical term for searching for the first sliver of the new moon.</term> of the moon, for the moon corrupts the seasons and arrives ten days early every year. Therefore, they will corrupt the years for themselves and make an abominable day a day of testimony, and a day of uncleanness a day of festival. They will mix everything together: holy days with the unclean, and unclean days with the holy.

<meta>This page continues the divine revelation to Moses concerning the corruption of the calendar. The previous page ended with a warning that the children of Israel would follow the errors of the Gentiles and forget the proper timing of the festivals. This page specifies how they will err regarding the Sabbath and Jubilees before transitioning into the story of Noah’s vineyard on Mount Lubar, which serves as a historical precedent for sacrificial laws.</meta>

...the holy ones¹ <note>original: "ቅዱሳን" (qeddusan)</note>, for they will go astray regarding the **new moons**, the **Sabbaths**, the **festivals**, and the **Jubilees**. And for this reason, I have commanded you and I testify to you so that you may testify to them, <margin>300 60 4</margin> because after your death your children will corrupt [the timing], so that they *do not observe the year* <margin>modern</margin> of **1364 days**² <note>The text here reads 1,364, though the standard Jubilees calendar is 364 days; this may be a scribal error or a specific manuscript variant.</note> as the only days [of the year]. And because of this, they will go astray regarding the new moon, the Sabbath, the seasons, and the festivals, and they will eat all kinds of blood with all kinds of meat.

And in the seventh week, in its first year, in this [period] Noah planted a vineyard <note>This occurs in the year 1317 Anno Mundi according to the Jubilee chronology.</note> on the mountain where the ark had rested, which is named **Lubar**, one of the mountains of **Ararat**. and it produced fruit³ in the fourth year. He guarded its fruit and harvested it in that year in the seventh month. And he made wine from it and put it into a vessel, and he kept it until the *fifth year*⁵, until the *first day* of the *first month*⁶. And he celebrated this day as a **festival day**⁷ with rejoicing. 

He prepared a burnt offering for the Lord: one young bull from the herd, one ram, and seven sheep, each one year old, and one kid of the goats, so that he might make atonement with it for himself and for his sons. And he prepared his offering... <margin><unclear>wo (rere</unclear></margin> and he placed some of its blood upon the meat of the sacrifice that he had made; and all the fat <margin><unclear>שנה</unclear></margin> <note>original Hebrew: "Shanah" (Year)</note> he offered up on the altar where he made the burnt offering *to the Lord*⁸. [He offered] the bull, the ram, and the sheep, and he offered all their meat upon the altar. 

And he placed all their offerings⁹ mixed with oil upon their peace offerings¹⁰; and after that, he sprinkled wine into the fire which was upon the altar...

For it was because of these three things that the flood came upon the earth: because of the fornication which the Watchers <term>Watchers<note>The "Watchers" (Teguhan) are a class of angels who, according to ancient tradition, abandoned heaven to consort with human women.</note></term> committed—straying from the command of their law—following the daughters of men. They took wives for themselves from all¹⁰ whom they chose, and they committed the first acts of uncleanness¹¹; and they fathered children, the Nephilim¹² <note>The "Nephilim" were the giant offspring of angels and humans.</note>. 

And these giants were not like one another; they began to devour each other. The Garbah¹³ <note>A specific class of giant in the Ethiopic tradition.</note> killed the Naphil, and the Naphil killed the Eljo, and the Eljo killed the children of humanity. Every person was turned against another, and everyone dedicated themselves¹⁴ to performing injustice and to shedding much blood¹⁵ <note>Variant T: "pure blood"</note>. Thus the earth was filled with injustice. And after all these, the wild beasts¹⁶, the cattle¹⁶, the birds, and everything that moves or stirs upon the earth did the same, and much blood was poured out upon the earth. Every thought and desire of man was to think only of vanity...

And he divided the earth into the regions which his three sons were to possess. And they reached out their hands and took for themselves a document <note>A written deed or "book" of their inheritance</note> from the hand of Noah, their father. <meta>This page continues the genealogy following the death of Noah and the division of the earth. Her name was **Lomona**, daughter of **Shinar**, and she bore him a son in the fourth year of this **week of years**. And he called his name **Peleg** <note>original: "Falek." Footnote 2 in the source notes a variant reading: "through their own counsel."</note>, so that they build for themselves a city and a tower in the land of **Shinar**."

For they migrated from the land of **Ararat** <note>original: "Ararat," where Noah's ark rested.</note> toward the east into **Shinar**; for afterwards they built the city and the tower, saying: "Let us ascend by it into heaven."

And while they were building, in the fourth week of years... <note>original: "the text is missing a verb here; variant T suggests 'they hurried' or 'they divided'."</note> they prepared the bricks with fire, and the **kiln** <term>kiln: 'ganfal' — a furnace used for firing bricks to make them as hard as stone.</term> baked them like stone. They joined them together with **bitumen** <term>bitumen: 'asphaltos' — a naturally occurring petroleum-based tar used as mortar.</term> which comes out of the sea and from the springs of water in the land of **Shinar**.

And they built it for **43 years**. For **5 years** they were forming the bricks. Its width was **14** bricks, and its thickness was **5** bricks. For the kiln, each side was **52 and 52 and 50...** <note>original: "The numbers and dimensions here are difficult to reconstruct; they represent the massive scale of the kiln and the tower's ascent."</note> and the height of the kiln was **40** cubits, and its breadth was **span-measure**, and **15 stages**.

And **the Lord our God** said to us <note>The "us" refers to the angels of the presence who are narrating the story to Moses.</note>: "Behold, the people are one and they have begun to act; and now, nothing will be withheld from them. Come, **let us go down** and confuse their tongues, so that no one understands the speech of his neighbor, and they shall be scattered into cities and nations, and no single counsel shall be established among them until the **Day of Judgment**."

And the Lord descended, and we descended with Him, to see the city and the tower which the children of men had built. And He **confused** <note>original: "botama." Variant 13 suggests "split" or "tore."</note> all the speech of their languages, and they no longer understood one another's speech. So they ceased from building the city and the tower. For this reason, the whole land of **Shinar** was named **Babel** <note>Babel: meaning "Confusion."</note>, because there the Lord confused all the languages of the children of men, and from there they were scattered into all their cities.

<meta>This page continues the story of the young Abraham’s miraculous intervention during a time of agricultural crisis. He spoke <note>original: "yinebbi," which can mean to prophesy or command</note> to the **crows** <note>original: "qi’at"</note> and said to them, before they could even alight upon the field, "Do not descend! And in the fifth year of the seventh week <term>Week: a seven-year period in the Jubilee calendar</term>, **Abraham** taught those who made the tools for the oxen—the woodworkers—and he crafted a device on top of the **plow** <term>original: "mayar"; refers to the furrow-making tool</term>. He placed it opposite the **handle of the plow** <term>original: "degure erf"</term> so that the seed could be cast into it. The seed would then descend from within the device into the center of the furrow and be hidden deep within the earth. In this way, they no longer had to fear the crows. 

He fashioned these devices for every plow-handle, and they sowed the land according to everything **Abraham** had commanded them. Thus, they no longer lived in fear of the birds.

<note>original footnote 1: Manuscript T reads "Evening/sunset" (Serka)</note>

# The Departure of Abram

And his father, Terah, said to him: "Go in peace. May the **God of the world** <note>original: "Amlaka Alam"</note>†¹ make your path straight, and may the Lord be with you and protect you from all evil. May He bestow upon you grace, mercy, and favor in the presence of those who ² see you, and may no child of man have power *over you³ to do *evil⁴; go in peace. And when you see a land that is pleasant to your eyes to look upon, then look [well], and come and take me *with you⁵ to yourself. And take Lot with you, the son of *Haran⁶ your brother, as your own son; and may the Lord be with you. But let Nahor your brother remain with us here until you return in peace, and then we shall all go with you together⁷."

<margin>12</margin> And Abram went out from Haran, *and he took Sarai⁸ his wife, and Lot, the son of Haran his brother, into the land of Canaan. And he came into the land of Asshur, and proceeded as far as Shechem, *and he dwelt¹⁰ near a tall oak <note>likely the Oak of Moreh, a significant landmark in Genesis</note>. *And he saw the land, and behold, it was very pleasant from the entry of Hamath unto the high mountain¹¹. And the Lord said to him: "To you and to your seed I will give this land." And he built an altar there and offered upon it a burnt offering to the Lord who had appeared to him. And he moved from there to the mountain of Bethel, with the sea <note>meaning the West</note> on one side and Ai on the side of the *sunrise¹², and he pitched his tent there. And he saw, and behold, the land was pleasant and broad¹³ *and very good¹⁴, and everything grew *upon it¹⁵: vines, and figs, and pomegranates; oak trees, and evergreen oaks, and terebinth trees; and olive trees, and cedars, and cypresses, and all the trees of the field; and there was great water upon the mountains. *And the Lord blessed¹⁶ him.

<meta>This page continues the aftermath of the battle of the kings. Abram has just been blessed by Melchizedek (implied by the start of the priestly discourse) and is now interacting with the King of Sodom before receiving a renewed vision from God. This text is part of the Book of Jubilees, which retells Genesis with a specific focus on sacred calendars and the origins of Jewish law.</meta>

God established an eternal ordinance regarding the nature of the priests: those who serve before Him are to *keep it¹* forever. There is no end to this law, *for it is²* an eternal ordinance for all generations, that they should give a **tithe** <note>The practice of giving a tenth of one's produce to God</note> to the Lord from the seed, from the wine, from the oil, from the cattle, and from the sheep. And He gave [these portions] to His priests to eat and to drink with joy *before Him³*. 

Then the King of Sodom came to him [Abram] and prostrated himself before him, saying: "Our lord Abram, give me the people whom you have rescued, but the cattle and the spoil which you have taken, let them be yours." 

And Abram said to him: "I lift my hand to the Most High God, the creator of heaven and earth, [swearing] that *I will not take⁴* anything of yours, so that *you may not⁵* say, 'I made Abram rich.' After these things, in the fourth year of this week, on the first day of the third month, <margin>14</margin> <note>The number 14 in the margin refers to the 14th Jubilee cycle in the book's chronology</note> the word of the Lord came to Abram *in a dream⁷*, saying: "Do not fear, Abram, *for⁸* I *am⁹* your protector, and your reward shall be *exceedingly¹⁰* great." 

And he said: "Lord, Lord, what will you give me? For I am passing away without *a child¹¹*; the son of *Meshek¹²*, the son of *my handmaid¹³*—that is Eliezer of Damascus—he is the one who will inherit from me, for you have given me no offspring." 

And He said to him: "This man shall not be your heir; only one who comes from your own body shall inherit from you."

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the covenant of circumcision from the Book of Jubilees, following the promise of Isaac and the blessing of Ishmael. It describes the immediate fulfillment of the command by Abraham and establishes the eternal theological significance of the rite as a marker of the elect, linking the people of Israel to the heavenly angelic orders.</meta>

...the **flesh of their foreskins.**¹ And at that time on that day, **Abraham was circumcised**³ and all the men of his house,⁴ and all those he had bought with gold from among the sons of foreigners were circumcised⁵ with him. This is a law for all generations forever; there is no skipping⁶ of days and no neglecting even a single day out of the eight days, for it is an eternal ordinance, ordained and written on the **Tablets of Heaven.** <term>Tablets of Heaven: *ṣellāta samāy* — in this tradition, these are heavenly records of divine laws and destiny that existed before the world was created.</term>

And every child who is born who is **not circumcised**⁷ in the flesh of his foreskin by the eighth day does not belong to the children of the ordinance which the Lord covenanted with Abraham, but rather⁸ is from the children of corruption. There is no sign upon him to show that he belongs to the Lord, for [he is destined] for destruction and for being blotted out from the earth, and for being uprooted from the earth,⁹ because he has broken the covenant of the Lord **our God.**¹⁰ 

For all the **Angels of the Presence** <term>Angels of the Presence: *malāʾekta gaṣṣ* — the highest rank of angels who serve in the immediate presence of God’s face</term> and all the **Angels of Holiness**—He created them thus from the day of their creation. And He looked upon the Angels of Holiness for the Holy One,¹¹ and He sanctified¹² Israel so they might be with Him and with His holy angels. 

And you, command the children of Israel that they observe the sign of this covenant for their generations¹³ as an eternal ordinance, so they are not uprooted from the earth. For the commandment is ordained as a covenant, so that they might observe it¹⁴ forever among all the children of Israel. 

For as for Ishmael and his children and his brothers and Esau, the Lord **did not bring them near**¹⁵ to Himself, nor did He choose them, even though they are the children of Abraham; for He knew them...

<meta>This page continues the divine instructions given to Moses regarding the covenant of circumcision. It contrasts the status of Israel, who are under God's direct governance, with the other nations of the world who are subject to subordinate spirits. It concludes with a warning about the dire spiritual consequences for those who neglect this rite, before transitioning into the narrative of the angels visiting Abraham.</meta>

But Israel He chose to be His people^1^ <gloss>original: "ሕዝበ" (ḥezba) — a distinct group or nation</gloss> and He sanctified them and gathered them from among all the children of men. For there are many nations and many peoples, and all of them belong to Him. But over all of them He has given authority^2^ to spirits to lead them astray from following Him. But over Israel He did not give authority,^3^ neither to any angel nor to any spirit. 

For He alone is their ruler, and He is the one who preserves them and seeks them out from the hand of His angels and from His spirits and from the hand of all. And [He gives] all His commandments so that He might preserve them^5^ and bless them, and that they might be His^6^ and He might be theirs from now and until eternity.^7^ 

And from now^8^ on I will declare to you that according to this ordinance <note>The rite of circumcision</note> the children of Israel^9^ will become negligent and will not circumcise^10^ their children according to the full requirement of the law. For in the flesh of their circumcision^11^ they leave behind^12^ that which is cut off^12^ of their children. And all the children of the night <note>original: "ውሉደ፡በሌሊት" (weluda balēlit). In other versions of Jubilees, this is "sons of Beliar" (demons/lawless ones). "Night" here may be a scribal variant or a metaphor for spiritual darkness.</note> leave their children without circumcision^14^ just as they were born. And there shall be a great death upon the children of Israel from the Lord, because they have forsaken His covenant^15^ and have turned away from the word and have rebelled and blasphemed, in that they do not perform this sign according to their ordinance.^16^ 

For they have made their spirits like the Gentiles <note>non-Israelite nations</note> to be removed^17^ and to be uprooted from the earth. And there is no longer for them life <term>Life: *ḥeyāwet* — spiritual existence or eternal salvation</term> and forgiveness, so that He might forgive and remit^18^ all the sins of this eternal error. 

And at the new moon of the fourth month, He appeared to Abraham at [the day/year] 17^19^...

64

...a kingdom, and a priesthood, and a holy people. And he set out on his journey and the path <note>original: "ፋኖተኒ" (fanotani) — his way or journeying</note> of his travel to the place of which we had told him. And they both rejoiced together <note>variant: "together" is added in manuscript T</note> with an exceedingly great joy. And there he built an altar to the Lord who had protected him and who had caused him to rejoice in the land of his migration <note>original: "ፋልሰቱ" (falsetu) — refers to his life as a resident alien or "sojourner"</note>. 

And he celebrated a great festival <note>original: "ፋሥሐ" (fasha) — while often meaning Passover, in this context it refers to a "festival of joy" celebrating Isaac's birth and weaning</note> in this month for seven days, near the altar which he had built by the Well of the Oath <note>original: "ሀዘቅተ፡ መሐላ" (Hazakta Mahala) — Beersheba</note>. And he built booths <note>original: "መጸላተ" (matsalata) — temporary shelters or tabernacles</note> for himself and for his servants during this festival. He was the first to celebrate the **Festival of Booths** <note>original: "በዓለ፡ መጸለት" (Ba'ala Matsalat) — also known as Sukkot or the Feast of Tabernacles</note> upon the earth. 

During these seven days, Abraham would offer sacrifices every day upon the altar of burnt offering <note>original: "መሠውዐ፡ ጽንሐሕ" (masaw'a tsinhah)</note> to the Lord: two bulls, seven sheep, and one kid of the goats for a sin offering to make atonement for himself and for his offspring. And for a peace offering, he sacrificed seven rams and seven sheep. And he would offer their fat upon the altar of burnt offering as a burnt sacrifice, a sweet-smelling savor <note>original: "መዐዛ፡ ሠናይ" (ma'aza sannay) — a pleasing aroma to God</note> to the Lord. 

In the morning and in the evening, he burned incense consisting of seven aromatics: **frankincense**, **galbanum** <note>original: "ቀነህተ" (qanahta)</note>, **stacte** <note>original: "ልባን" (liban)</note>, **nard**, **myrrh**, **spice** <note>original: "ሰንበላቴ" (sanbalate) — likely spikenard or cinnamon</note>, and **costus** <note>original: "ቀስጠ" (qasta)</note>. All seven of these he offered, ground fine, mixed equally, and pure. And he celebrated this festival for seven days, rejoicing with all his heart and with all his soul—he and everyone in his household. And there was no stranger with him, nor anyone who was uncircumcised. And he blessed and praised his Creator who had created him in his generation...

For by His own good pleasure He created him, because He knew and determined that from him would come a **plant of righteousness** for the eternal generations, and from him a holy seed, so that they might be like the One who made everything. # 67

...for my child." And she sat and wept. And an angel of God, one of the holy ones <note>original: "እምቅዱሳን" (em-qedusan) — referring to the class of holy angels who serve in the divine presence</note>, said to her: "What causes you to weep, Hagar? And it happened in the seventh week, in the first year, in the first month of this **Jubilee** <term>Jubilee: A period of 49 years used in this manuscript to track sacred history</term>, in that month, there were words in the heavens concerning Abraham, that he was faithful in everything that He spoke, and that God loved him because he was faithful in every trial.

And **Mastema** <note>original: "ምስቴማ" (Mastema) — the chief of the spirits who acts as an accuser and tempter, similar to the figure of Satan in the Book of Job</note> came before us and said before God: "Behold, Abraham loves Isaac his son, and he favors him above all else. Tell him to offer him as a burnt offering upon the altar, and You will see if he performs this word; then You will know if he is faithful in everything in which he is commanded."

And God knew that Abraham was faithful in every trial which He had told him. For I have tested him by his wealth and by the land, and I tested him by the word of the kings, and I tested him again by his wife when she was taken away, and by his exile, and I tested him by Ishmael and by Hagar his handmaid when he sent them away. In everything in which I tested him, he was found faithful.

And he arrived at the place <gloss>*</gloss> of water<gloss>⁹ Manuscript A says "of the oath"</gloss>, and he said to his young men, “Stay here with the donkey, and I and the lad will go <gloss>*</gloss> and worship<gloss>¹⁰ omitted in Manuscript T</gloss>, and after we have worshipped, we will return to you.”

And he took the wood of the sacrifice and laid it upon Isaac his son, and he took in his hands the fire and the knife, and the two of them went together to that place. Do not leave any for the third day <gloss>¹⁰ Manuscript A: "You shall eat it on the third day"; Manuscript E: "For you shall not eat it on the third day"</gloss>, because <gloss>¹¹ Manuscript A: "If indeed"; Manuscript E: "If indeed it be"</gloss> it will not be accepted, for it is no longer a chosen [offering], and do not eat it thereafter; for all who eat it shall take sin upon themselves.

For thus I have found it written in the books of my ancestors who came before me, in the words of Enoch and in the words of Noah <gloss>¹² Manuscript A: "and Noah"</gloss>. And in all <gloss>¹³ Manuscript T omits "all"</gloss> your sacrifices, you shall sprinkle salt; and let the covenant of salt <term>Covenant of salt: An ancient metaphor for a permanent and unbreakable agreement, as salt was a preservative that prevented decay.</term> never be lacking <gloss>¹⁴ Manuscript A: "And it shall not cease"</gloss> in all your sacrifices <gloss>¹⁵ Manuscript A adds: "that you may sprinkle salt"</gloss>.

<summary>Abraham provides Isaac with detailed instructions on sacrificial rituals, emphasizing the proper handling of blood and fat, and citing the ancient writings of Enoch and Noah. He warns that failing to follow these dietary and sacrificial laws results in sin.</summary>

# 78

And that [the blood] which is poured out, let it be drained into the soil <note>The original Ge'ez "ለመርር" is difficult to translate; the editor suggests it may refer to "bitterness" or the ground. Note 1: Manuscript T reads "and his blood," while the editor suggests it could be "and bitter."</note> and do not eat their blood. Be pure from the blood of humans <note>Note 2: Manuscript A adds "to."</note>, [shedding it] without cause <note>Note 3: Manuscripts A and T read "by/in the blood."</note>. For those who have shed [blood] and have believed in human blood—the blood of guilt—you shall be held accountable <note>Note 4: Manuscript A reads "human, because the blood is received."</note> before the Lord <note>Note 5: Manuscript A adds "to."</note>, the God Most High. 

His protection is for the good; if He grants it, you shall be protected from all evil, and if He grants it, He will save you from all death. Behold, I see, my son, that all the deeds of the children of men are like sin and evil, and all their works are filthy, contemptible, and corrupt. There is no righteousness with them. Take heed; do not walk in their paths, the paths of those who destroy <note>Note 6: Manuscript A reads "And for those who destroy into."</note> their footprints. Do not err with an error leading to death <note>Note 7: Manuscript A reads "deviation."</note> before God Most High, lest He turn His face from you and deliver you into the power of your own transgressions. 

Then He will uproot you from the earth, and your seed from under heaven, and your name and your seed will be blotted out from all the earth. Rise up from all their works and from all their filth, and keep the charge <note>Note 8: Manuscript T uses the plural "they kept."</note> of the God Most High. Do His will and be upright in all things, and He will bless you in all your works. And He will raise up from you a <term>Plant of Righteousness</term> <note>A common biblical metaphor (found in Enoch and Jubilees) referring to a holy lineage or the people of Israel who will flourish in truth.</note> throughout the whole earth and through all <note>Note 9: Manuscript A adds "and in all."</note> the generations of the earth.

And my name and your name shall be known <note>Note 10: Manuscript A reads "it shall not be hidden"; the editor suggests reading "it shall be known."</note> under heaven for all time. Go, my son, in peace. May the God Most High—my God and your God—strengthen you to do His will. And may He bless all your seed <note>Note 11: Manuscript A adds "to."</note> and the remnant of your seed for generations of generations forever, with every blessing of righteousness, so that you may be a blessing on all the earth.

And he [Isaac] went out from him <note>Note 12: Manuscript A reads "with him."</note>, rejoicing <note>Note 13: Manuscript T adds "to."</note> and taking his leave.

And it happened in the seventh week of the first year, in the 150th Jubilee, in the second year—which was the twenty-second year [of that cycle] in which Abraham died—that Isaac and Ishmael came from the Well of the Oath <note>The Hebrew name is Beer-sheba.</note> to where they celebrate the **Feast of Weeks** <note>Note 1: Manuscript A reads "of seven"; Manuscript T reads "of these seven." # 80

...my children, all the days of my life until this day. May my God be merciful¹ upon your servant and upon the seed of his children³ so that they might be² a chosen people and an inheritance from among all the nations <note>The Ge'ez term "mayar" used here is a difficult technical term in the Ethiopic version of Jubilees, likely meaning "beloved" or "specially chosen."</note> from now and for all the generations of the world for all ages.

And he called Jacob and said to him, "My son⁴ Jacob, may the God of all bless you and strengthen you⁵ to perform righteousness and his will before him⁶ ⁷. And he shall choose you and your seed so that you may be for him a people of inheritance according to his own will for all time. And you, my son Jacob, draw near and kiss me."

And he drew near and kissed him, and he said: "Blessed be Jacob, my son,⁸ and all his children to the Lord for all the ages. May the Lord give you a seed of righteousness; and from your children may some be sanctified⁹ above all the <term>mayar</term> <note>original: "መያር" (mayar). This word appears frequently in the Ethiopic Jubilees but its exact origin is debated; it functions here as a title for a special, beloved class of people.</note>. And all nations shall serve you¹⁰ and bow down before your seed. 

Be strong in the presence of men, and you shall have dominion¹¹ over all the seed of the beloved and over the seed¹² of Seth. Then your paths and the paths¹³ of your children shall be made righteous in peace [as] a holy people¹⁴. May the God of all give you the blessings¹⁵ with which he blessed me, and with which he blessed Noah and Abraham. May your holy seed rest¹⁶ ... for all generations¹⁷ and forever¹⁸. And may the Lord¹⁹ cleanse you from all uncleanness and injustice, so that you may make atonement for all the sins you committed in ignorance and for your errors. And may he strengthen you²⁰ and bless you always, all [your days]..."

# 83

...all the days of the ages <gloss>1</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "worlds."</note>, and You have renewed Your covenant and showed mercy with him and with his offspring through all <gloss>2</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "and in all."</note> Your subtle graces until <gloss>3</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "and in."</note> all the generations of the earth.

And he <note>Abraham</note> placed the two fingers of Jacob upon his own eyes, and he blessed nineteen <note>Likely a verse or division marker in this manuscript tradition.</note> the God of gods; then he covered his face and stretched out his feet and slept the eternal sleep, and was gathered to his fathers <gloss>4</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "and they were gathered to," while Manuscript E adds: "his children."</note>. And through all this, Jacob was lying in his bosom and did not realize that Abraham, his father’s <gloss>5</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "to."</note> father, had died. 

Then Jacob awoke from his sleep, and behold, Abraham was as cold as frost <note>The Ge'ez word "barad" can mean hail, snow, or frost, emphasizing the coldness of death.</note>. And he rose from his <gloss>7</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "to."</note> bosom and ran and told Rebecca, his mother. And Isaac fell upon the face of his father <gloss>10</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "to."</note> Abraham, and he wept and blessed him <gloss>11</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "and he arose," which appears to be a scribal error.</note> and kissed him. And he wept <gloss>14</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "and they wept."</note> for Abraham his father <gloss>15</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "to."</note>—he <gloss>16</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "house."</note> and all the household of Abraham—and they wept with a great weeping.

And his sons Isaac and Ishmael buried him in the cave of the double tomb <note>The "Cave of Machpelah," the traditional burial site of the patriarchs.</note> near Sarah his wife. And they mourned for him fifty-nine <note>Most versions of the Book of Jubilees specify forty days of mourning; fifty-nine is a variant in this specific manuscript.</note> days—all the people of his house, and Isaac, and Ishmael, and all their children, and the children of Keturah <note>Abraham’s wife after Sarah’s death.</note> in their places. And the mourning and weeping for Abraham <gloss>17</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "weeping of Abraham."</note> was completed.

And he lived one hundred and seventy-five years—seventy <note>The text says "seventy weeks of years," but based on the total of 175, this is a scribal error for "twenty-five weeks of years" (25 x 7 = 175).</note> weeks of years, making 175 years. And the days were completed...

# 84

He lived his life and grew very old in days <gloss>1</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "his days."</note>. For as for the days of the first ones, their lives were nineteen <term>Jubilees</term> <note>In this context, a "Jubilee" refers to a 49-year cycle (seven weeks of years). The text is comparing the long lives of the earliest ancestors to later generations.</note>; but after them, the days of their lives began to diminish from nineteen Jubilees, and they aged quickly <gloss>2</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "from the Jubilees."</note>. Their days were cut short because of much suffering and the evil of their ways—except for Abraham. For Abraham was perfect in all his deeds <gloss>3</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "his deed."</note> with God, and he was preserved in his righteousness <gloss>4</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "in his righteousness."</note> all the days of his life. And behold, he completed nine Jubilees of life, until he reached old age <gloss>5</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "for he found" or "when he found."</note>, the time of a weary countenance, and he was sated with his days.

And every generation that arises from now until the day of the great judgment will grow old quickly, before they even complete two Jubilees. Their knowledge will fail <gloss>6</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "it will be."</note> because of their premature aging <gloss>7</gloss> <note>Manuscript A adds: "of."</note>, and all their understanding will depart. In those days, if a man lives a Jubilee and a half <gloss>8</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "years."</note>, they will say of him, "He has lived long," yet the majority of his days will be filled with suffering, toil, and distress, and there will be no peace. For plague shall be upon plague, and strife <gloss>9</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "and strifes."</note> upon strife, and distress upon distress, and evil <gloss>10</gloss> <note>The text here is partially broken or abbreviated.</note> upon evil, and sickness <gloss>11</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "with."</note> upon sickness, and every kind of evil judgment like these: disease and tumors <gloss>12</gloss> <note>Manuscript A uses a slightly different spelling for "tumors."</note>, and frost, and hail, and fever, and lightnings <gloss>13</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "and flashes of lightning."</note>, and stinging heat <gloss>14</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "and stinging heat."</note>, and sickness <gloss>15</gloss> <note>Manuscript A adds: "to."</note>, and parched earth <gloss>16</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "and parched earth."</note>, and pestilence, and death, and calamity, and captivity, and every [plague...]

# 85

...and pain and visions <note>original: ሕልም (ḥəlm) - literally 'dreams', but often implies visions of distress or hallucinations in this context</note>. And they will say concerning the first ones and their days, "They were many, up to twelve years" <gloss>5</gloss> <note>Manuscript A adds "to." And in that generation, children will be found *rebuking* <gloss>8</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "they will be rebuked."</note> their fathers and their elders because of sin, and because of violence, and because of the speech of their mouths, and because of the great evils <gloss>9</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "evil ones."</note> they commit, and because they have forsaken the ordinance which the Lord <gloss>10</gloss> <note>Manuscript A adds "to."</note> established between them. They have no one to discipline them, nor do they follow the law. *For* <gloss>11</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "until."</note> they have all acted wickedly, and every mouth speaks transgression. All their works are impure and abominable, and all their ways are crooked and impure.

They shall perish because of all their deeds. There shall be no "seed of the eye" <note>This is an idiom likely meaning 'mercy' or a 'remnant'—one who remains in the sight of God.</note> and no anointing, for all their works are filled with fraud. They shall all perish together—wild beasts, cattle, birds, and all the fish of the sea—because of the children of men. And they shall fight among themselves, those who are with these: the young with the old <gloss>12</gloss> <note>Manuscript T adds "until."</note>, and the old with the young, the poor with the wealthy, the lowly with the great, and the needy with the official, concerning the Law and concerning the Covenant. And they shall stand...

The path of righteousness. <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: ወእምዝ (wä-’əmzə) - Manuscript A follows this with an imperfect verb form.</note> And after this, the days shall increase and grow long, and the children of men, <gloss>2</gloss> <note>Manuscript A simply reads "generation."</note> generation after generation, and age after age, until the time their days approach one thousand years, <gloss>3</gloss> <note>Manuscript T reads: "and for many years."</note> and for many years and many days. # 88

A famine <gloss>1</gloss> <note>Manuscript A uses the preposition "for" or "to" before famine.</note> occurred in the days of Abraham, and Jacob cooked a stew of lentils. <term>stew of lentils: ተብሲለ፡ ብርኩዝ (täbsilä bərkuz) — "bərkuz" refers specifically to pulses or lentils, reflecting the "red pottage" of the Genesis narrative.</term> Then Esau came in from the field, hungry, and he said to his brother Jacob, "Give me some of this wheat stew." <note>original: ተብሲለ፡ ዘሠርናይ (täbsilä zä-śärnay). Interestingly, the text here shifts from "lentils" to "wheat" (śärnay) when describing the stew Esau requested.</note> Jacob replied to him, "Give back to me your birthright <gloss>2</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "your seniority of the first birth."</note> which is the seniority of birth, and I will give you bread and some of this wheat stew." <gloss>3</gloss> <note>Manuscript A adds: "and if indeed it is the stew."</note> 

Esau said, "Look, I am going to die; of what use is this birthright <gloss>4</gloss> <note>Manuscript A: "this being born first."</note> to me?" Jacob said to him, "Swear an oath to me," and Jacob said <gloss>5</gloss> <note>Manuscript A uses the preposition "to."</note>, "Swear to me today." <gloss>6</gloss> <note>Manuscript T contains an unclear variant, possibly meaning "to be weary."</note> So he swore to him. Then Jacob gave his brother Esau bread and stew, and he ate until he was satisfied. Thus Esau despised his birthright <gloss>7</gloss> <note>Manuscript A: "being first."</note>. For this reason, Esau was named Edom, because of the wheat stew that Jacob gave him in exchange for his birthright. <gloss>8</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "for the first." Manuscript T reads: "for the firstborn."</note> Jacob then grew in stature, while Esau was brought low from his position of honor.

Then a famine came upon the land, and Isaac <gloss>9</gloss> <note>Manuscript A adds the preposition "to/for."</note> intended to go down to Egypt during the passing year of this week <term>week: ሱባዔ (suba'e) — in the Book of Jubilees, time is measured in "weeks" of seven years each.</term>. He traveled to the King of the Philistines in Gerar, to Abimelech.

And the Lord appeared to him and said, "Do not go down to Egypt. Dwell in the land of which I tell you <gloss>10</gloss> <note>Manuscript A reads: "which I give to you."</note>. Sojourn in this land, and I will be with you and bless you; for to you and your offspring I will give all this land. I will establish my oath which I swore to your father Abraham. I will multiply your offspring like the stars <gloss>11</gloss> <note>Manuscript A uses the singular "star."</note> of heaven, and I will give all this land to your offspring. And through your offspring, all the nations of the earth shall be blessed, because your father <gloss>12</gloss> <note>Manuscript A: "your father who heard."</note> heard my voice and kept my word <gloss>13</gloss> <note>Manuscript A: "the keeper of my word."</note>, my commandments, my laws, and my statutes, and me. And now, hear my voice and dwell [here]." <gloss>14</gloss> <note>Manuscript T adds: "and my commandment."</note>
