# 89

into *Zamayer*¹ <note>original: ዛመይር (Zamäyər). The meaning is debated; it may refer to the specific duration or end of the famine mentioned on the previous page. Manuscript A reads "Mäyər."</note> and he lived in Gerar for one "week" of years <note>The Book of Jubilees measures time in "weeks" (7 years) and "jubilees" (49 years). Isaac stayed here for seven years.</note>. And Abimelech gave orders concerning him and concerning all that was his, saying: "Any man who touches him or anything that is his shall surely die." And Isaac grew great in Philistia, and he came to possess many belongings: cattle, sheep, camels, donkeys, and much wealth. *And he sowed*² in the land of the Philistines and harvested a hundredfold. Isaac was blessed <term>blessed: ፈያፋያ (fäya-faya) — an ancient Ge'ez term used here to describe God making Isaac "exceedingly great" or "prosperous."</term>, and the Philistines became envious of him. And all the *wells*³ which the servants of Abraham had dug during the life of Abraham, the Philistines blocked them after Abraham died and filled them with earth. 

And Abimelech said to Isaac: "Go away from us, for you have become much more powerful than us." And Isaac departed from there in the fourth year of the seventh "week" <note>This corresponds to a specific date in the jubilee calendar system used by the author.</note> and moved into the valley of Gerar. And they returned and dug the wells of water which the servants of Abraham his father⁴ had dug, and which the Philistines had stopped up after the death of Abraham his father⁴; and he called them by the same names that Abraham his father had named them. 

And the servants of Isaac dug a *well*⁵ in the valley <note>original: ፋኖጋ (Fanoga), likely a transliteration of the Hebrew "nahal," meaning a dry riverbed or valley.</note> and found *living*⁶ water <term>living water: ማየ፡ ሕያው (mayä ḥəyaw) — a biblical term for flowing spring water, as opposed to stagnant cistern water.</term>. And the shepherds of Gerar strove with Isaac's shepherds, saying: "The water is ours." And Isaac called the name of that well "Hardship" <term>Hardship: ዕፁብ (ʿəḍub) — equivalent to the Hebrew "Esek," meaning strife or contention.</term> because they *strove*⁷ with us. *And they dug*⁸ another well, and they fought *over it*⁹; and *Isaac*¹⁰ called its name "Enmity" <term>Enmity: ጸባብ (ṣäbab) — equivalent to the Hebrew "Sitnah," meaning hostility or opposition.</term>. *And they rose up*¹¹ from there and *dug*⁸ another *well*⁵ and *did not strive*¹² over it. And he called its name "Room" <term>Room: ስርሕ (śərḥ) — equivalent to the Hebrew "Rehoboth," meaning wide spaces or broad paths.</term>. And Isaac said: "Now the Lord has made room for us, and *we shall increase*¹³ upon the earth."

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Isaac's life from page 89. Having moved away from the Philistine city of Gerar due to their envy, Isaac now settles in the valley, digs wells, and enters into a tense diplomatic treaty with the Philistine king, Abimelech. This section of Jubilees (Chapter 24) is a retelling of Genesis 26 but adds a harsh prophetic curse against the Philistines.</meta>

# 90

And he moved from there into the valley and settled there in the first year of the first "week" of the fourth jubilee, in the one hundred and twentieth year <note>In the Jubilees calendar, this is the year 2108 from creation.</note>. And God appeared to him on that night, on the new moon of the first month, and said to him: "I am the God of Abraham your father; do not fear, for I am with you, and I will bless you and I will surely multiply your seed as the sand of the sea for the sake of Abraham my servant."

And he built an altar there, just as Abraham his father had built one before, and he called upon the name of the Lord and offered a sacrifice to the God of Abraham his father. And they dug a well and found living water. And Isaac's servants also dug another well and did not find water; and they went and told Isaac that they had not found water. And Isaac said: "I have sworn on this day to the Philistines, and this thing has happened to us" <note>Isaac interprets the failure to find water in the second well as a spiritual sign or consequence of his deal with the Philistines.</note>. And Isaac called the name of that place "The Well of the Oath" <term>Well of the Oath: ሀዘቅት፡ወሐለ (Häzäqt Wäḥälä) — This is the Ge'ez translation of "Beersheba," the site where Isaac and Abimelech made their covenant.</term>, because there he had sworn an oath to Abimelech and to Ahuzzath his companion and to Phicol his general.

And Isaac knew on that day that they had sworn to him under duress <note>original: በገፋዕ (bägäfa‘) — implying they were pressured or acting with hidden motives rather than genuine friendship.</note> to make peace with them. And Isaac cursed the Philistines on that day and said: "Cursed be the Philistines for the day of wrath and anger! May God give them over to derision, and to a curse, and to wrath, and to anger by the hands of sinful nations, and into the hands of the Kittim <term>Kittim: ቤጤሞ (Betemo) — A biblical term for people from Cyprus or the Mediterranean coast, often used in later texts to refer to the Greeks or Romans who would eventually conquer the region.</term> who shall uproot them. And whoever escapes from the sword of the enemy and from the Kittim, a righteous nation shall uproot them in judgment from under heaven; for they shall be enemies and foes to my children throughout their days upon the earth. And no remnant shall be left to them, nor any to be saved on the day of the judgment of wrath."

# 91

For destruction, and for theft, and for removal from the earth—all the seed of the Philistines <note>original: "ፋስክ" (Fasäk). In the Book of Jubilees, this term refers to the Philistines, the inhabitants of the coastal plain.</note> shall be uprooted. No person of any size <note>literally: "child of any width/extension."</note> shall remain for them, nor any name, and no remnant of his seed shall be left upon the earth. For even if he should ascend to heaven <note>variation: "even if he should ascend like a bird."</note>, from there He will bring him down. And even if he strengthens himself in the earth, from there He will drag him out. And even if he hides himself among the nations, from there also He will uproot him. And even if he goes down into Sheol <term>Sheol: ሲኦል (Si’ol) — The underworld or the place of the dead.</term>, there also his judgment shall be increased, and he shall have no peace there. And even if he goes into captivity at the hands of those who seek his life, on the way they shall kill him. No name or seed will be left for him in all the earth, for he shall walk into an eternal curse. And thus it was written and engraved concerning him upon the heavenly tablets, to perform it for him on the day of judgment, so that he might be uprooted from the earth.

And in the second year of this week <term>week: ሱባዔ (Suba‘e) — A unit of seven years in the specific calendar used in the Book of Jubilees.</term>, in this jubilee, Rebekah called Jacob her son and spoke to him, saying: "My son, do not take for yourself a wife from the daughters of Canaan, like Esau your brother, who took for himself two wives from the seed of Canaan. And they have embittered my spirit <note>variation: "my soul."</note> with all their filthy deeds; for all their deeds are of fornication and impurity, and there is no righteousness with them, for they are evil. And I, my son, have loved you very much, and may my God of mercy <note>Or "my son of mercy," a term of endearment.</note> bless you at all times and be the guardian of my word. And now, my son, hear my voice and do the will of your mother..."

<meta>This page continues the narrative from the Book of Jubilees (corresponding to Genesis 27), immediately following Isaac’s blessing of Jacob. The text transitions from Jacob’s departure to Esau’s return from the field and the subsequent discovery of the deception.</meta>

And Esau his brother arrived from his hunting,<sup>1</sup> and he also made food and brought it to his father. And he said to his father, "Arise,<sup>3</sup> my father, and eat<sup>4</sup> of my hunt,<sup>5</sup> so that your soul may bless me." 

And Isaac his father said to him, "Who are you?" 

And he said to him, "I am your firstborn, Esau, and I have done as you commanded me." 

And Isaac was struck with a very great trembling<sup>6</sup> and said, "Who then is he who went out hunting<sup>7</sup> and provided for me and brought it, and I ate of it all before you came? And I blessed him—and blessed shall he be, and all his seed forever!" 

And it happened when Esau heard the words of his father Isaac, he cried out with a very great and bitter<sup>8</sup> voice and said to his father, "Bless me, even me also, O father!" 

And he said to him, "Your brother came with guile <term>guile (በጕሕል): craftiness or deceit</term> and took your blessing." 

.........<sup>9</sup> And he [Esau] said, "Is he not rightly named Jacob? <term>Heavenly Tablets: In the Book of Jubilees, this refers to a celestial library where all laws, historical events, and destinies are eternally recorded by God.</term>
<term>Wedding feast: The Ge'ez "gizyan" refers to the traditional seven-day celebration period for a marriage.</term>
<note>Tahsas: The fourth month of the Ethiopian calendar, roughly corresponding to December. In the context of Jubilees, this aligns the patriarchs' lives with the solar calendar.</note>
<term>Third week: A "week" in this text is a period of seven years. The "third week" refers to the third set of seven years within a 49-year Jubilee cycle.</term>

And he crossed the River <note>This refers to the Euphrates River</note> and reached the land of **Gilead**. And **Jacob** hid his intention from **Laban** and did not tell him.

And in the seventh year of the fourth **week** <term>week: in the Jubilees calendar, a seven-year sabbatical cycle</term>, **Jacob** turned toward **Gilead** in the first month, on the twenty-fifth day. And **Laban** went to pursue him and caught up with **Jacob** on Mount **Gilead** in the third month, on the twenty-seventh day. And God did not allow him to do harm to **Jacob**, for He appeared to him in a dream at night. And **Laban** spoke to **Jacob**. And on the twenty-seventh day of that month, **Jacob** made a feast of remembrance <note>original: "ዝክረ," often referring to a commemorative meal or covenant meal</note> for **Laban** and for all those who came with him. And **Jacob** swore an oath to **Laban** on that day, and **Laban** likewise to **Jacob**.

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and theological interpretation of the Dina incident from the Book of Jubilees. The previous page detailed the violent retribution taken by Simeon and Levi against the city of Shechem. This page transitions from the narrative of the event to the establishment of an eternal law against intermarriage with "Gentiles" (non-Israelites), framing the act as a capital offense recorded in heaven.</meta>

And it was written in the **heavenly tablets** <note>The "heavenly tablets" are a recurring motif in Jubilees, representing a pre-existent divine blueprint of history and law that Moses is being shown.</note> concerning all the offspring of **Israel**:
he who defiles [a woman] shall surely die; he must be put to death, and they shall stone him with stones. And there is no time limit for this law, and no end, and no forgiveness; instead, the man who has defiled a daughter in the midst of all **Israel** shall be uprooted, for from his offspring he has given to **Molech** <note>original: "ሞሌክ" (Molek). While traditionally a deity associated with child sacrifice, the text here uses the term to equate intermarriage with the ultimate betrayal of the covenant.</note> and has sinned by defiling [his seed]. And you also, **Moses**, command the children of **Israel** and testify against them that they should not give their daughters to the **Gentiles** <note>term: 'አሕዛብ' (Aḥzāb) - meaning the surrounding nations or non-covenant peoples</note>, and that they should not take from the daughters of the Gentiles, for it is unclean before the Lord.

And for this reason I have written for you in the ^1 words of the law ^2 all the deeds of the **Sons of Levi** <note>The text likely refers to the "sons of Jacob" or the specific zeal of Levi mentioned on the previous page</note> which they performed regarding **Dinah**, and how the ^3 children of **Jacob** spoke, saying: "We will not give our daughter to a man who has a foreskin <note>term: 'ቁልፈት' (qulfat) - the state of being uncircumcised</note>, for that is a **disgrace** <note>original: 'ጽልመት' (tselmat) - literally 'darkness,' but used here to mean a 'reproach' or 'shame'</note> ^4 to us."

And it is a ^5 disgrace for **Israel** for those who give and for those who ^6 take daughters from the Gentiles; for it is unclean and detestable to **Israel**. And **Israel** shall not be cleansed ^7 from this uncleanness—the man who has a wife from the daughters of the Gentiles, or if he has given his daughters to a man from any of the Gentiles; for it is ^8 plague upon ^9 plague, curse upon curse, and ^10 every judgment and plague and curse ^11 shall come. And if a person ^12 does [this] word, or if his eyes are closed <note>A Hebrew-style idiom meaning to "ignore" or "tolerate" a sin</note> toward those who commit uncleanness and toward those who defile the sanctuary of the Lord and toward those who profane His holy name, the whole nation shall be judged together because of all who have defiled and neglected this; and there is no showing of partiality...

<meta>This page continues the discourse following the incident at Shechem. Text: And there is no partiality of face <note>This means God does not show favoritism or bias based on status.</note>, and there is no receiving from his hand a ransom, or a sacrifice, or incense, or a gift, or a sweet-smelling savor, that He should accept it; instead, [judgment] shall be upon every man and woman in **Israel** who defiles His sanctuary. And for this reason I have commanded you, saying: "Proclaim this testimony against **Israel**: See how it was for **Solomon** <note>original: 'ሰሎሞን' (Salomon). And the seed of **Levi** was chosen for the priesthood and for the **Levites**, so that they might minister before the **Lord** as we do <note>The "we" refers to the angels of the presence who are narrating the book to Moses.</note> at all times. And **Levi** and his children shall be blessed forever, because he was zealous to perform **righteousness** and judgment and vengeance upon all those who rose up against **Israel**. And thus let it be recorded for him as a testimony on the **heavenly tablets**: blessing and **righteousness** before the God of all. And we also remember the **righteousness** which a man has performed in his life at all times of the year, up to a thousand generations; let there be a **blessing**, and let it come to him and to his children after him, and let it be written as favor and **righteousness** on the **heavenly tablets**. And all these things I have written for you, and I have commanded you to tell the children of **Israel** that they should not commit sin, nor stumble against the ordinance, nor break the **covenant** which has been ordained for them so that they might perform it and be recorded as friends. But if they stumble and perform any of the ways of uncleanness, they shall be recorded on the **heavenly tablets** as enemies, and they shall be blotted out from the **book of the living**, and they shall be written in the book of those who are destroyed and with those who are uprooted from the earth on the day of the slaughter of the chil[dren...]

<meta>This page continues the aftermath of the events at Shechem. Following the discourse on Levi’s zeal and the divine recording of righteousness, the narrative shifts to the physical departure of Jacob’s family from the area and their spiritual purification before traveling to Bethel.</meta>

**Jacob** and his sons went up from **Shechem** <note>original: 'ሰቂሞን' (Sekimon). This refers to the city of Shechem, where the sons of Jacob had just taken revenge for the defilement of their sister Dinah.</note>, and it was written in heaven that he had performed **righteousness**, justice, and vengeance upon the sinners, and it was recorded for a blessing. And they brought out **Dinah**, their sister, from the house of **Shechem**. And they took everything that was in **Shechem**: their sheep, their cattle, their donkeys, all their wealth, and all their goods; and they brought everything to **Jacob**, their father.

And he spoke with them because they had killed [the people of] the city, for he was afraid <note>A variant reading suggests 'they were afraid.'</note> of those who dwelt in the land—the **Canaanites** and the **Perizzites**. But the terror of the **Lord** fell upon all the cities surrounding **Shechem**, and they did not rise up to pursue the children of **Jacob**, for they were struck with dread.

And on the first day of the month <note>The "New Moon," a significant time for new beginnings and religious observation in the ancient calendar.</note>, **Jacob** spoke to all the people of his house, saying: "Purify yourselves and change your clothes; and let us arise and go up to **Bethel**, where I prayed a prayer when I was fleeing from the face of **Esau**, my brother, who was with me; for [God] has brought me back into this land in peace. And put away the foreign gods that are among you."

And they gave up the foreign gods, and those [rings] which were in their ears, and those which were on their necks, and the idols which **Rachel** had stolen from **Laban**, her father; she gave them all to **Jacob**. And he burned them, and broke them to pieces, and destroyed them, and hid them under the **terebinth** <term>terebinth: A large, long-lived tree (Pistacia terebinthus) often used as a landmark or sacred site in the biblical landscape.</term> which was in the land of **Shechem**. And he went up on the first day of the seventh month to **Bethel**.

And he built an altar in the place where he had slept <note>Literally "where he stayed." This refers to the site of his famous dream of the ladder to heaven.</note>, and he set up a pillar there. And he sent word to his father **Isaac**, that he should come to him at his altar, and also to his mother, **Rebekah**. And **Isaac** said, "Let my son **Jacob** come, that I may see him before I die." And **Jacob** went to **Isaac**, his father.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Jacob's journey to visit his parents, Isaac and Rebecca, at Hebron. Having left Shechem and Bethel, Jacob now brings two of his sons—Levi and Judah—to receive the blessing of their grandfather, a pivotal moment in the establishment of the priestly and royal lines of Israel.</meta>

And he stayed with his mother **Rebecca** in the house of his father **Abraham**. Two of his children he took with him: **Levi** and **Judah**. They came to his father **Isaac**, to where his mother **Rebecca** lived. **Rebecca** came out from the room into the doorway of her dwelling to greet **Jacob** *and she embraced him¹*, for her spirit had been revived when she heard that her son **Jacob** had *arrived²*. She kissed him and saw his two children; she recognized them and said to him, "Are these your children, my son?" She embraced them, kissed them, and blessed them, saying, "Through you shall the seed of **Abraham** be honored, and you shall be *a blessing³* upon the earth."

Then **Jacob** entered the inner chamber to his father **Isaac**, where he was lying down, and his two children were with him. As [Jacob] took his father's *hand⁴* and, bowing down, kissed him⁵, **Isaac** hung upon the neck of his son **Jacob** and wept upon his neck. The *dimness⁶* disappeared from **Isaac's** eyes and he saw the two sons of **Jacob**, **Levi** and **Judah**. He said, "Are these your sons, my son? For they resemble you." [Jacob] told him, "They are indeed my sons; and you have *seen⁷* that they are truly my sons⁸." Then they drew near to him, and they turned, and he kissed them and embraced them *both together⁹*.

And the spirit of prophecy descended into his heart. He took **Levi** in his right hand and **Judah** in his left hand¹⁰. He turned toward **Levi** and began to bless him first, saying: "May the **Lord**, the **God** of all, who is the **Lord** of all ages, bless you and your children throughout all ages. May the **Lord** grant to you and your seed greatness *and high honor¹¹*, and may He bring you [near to Himself]..."

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Isaac blessing his grandsons, Levi and Judah, in the presence of Jacob and Rebecca. The previous page ended with Isaac beginning his blessing over Levi, establishing his future role as a priest and spiritual leader. This text is part of the Book of Jubilees, an ancient Jewish retelling of Genesis and Exodus.</meta>

"And your seed¹ shall they bring² near to Him from all flesh, so that they may serve in His sanctuary, like the **Angels of the Presence** <term>Angels of the Presence</term><note>High-ranking celestial beings who serve in the direct presence of God. In the Book of Jubilees, the priests are often compared to these angels because they both serve in a sacred sanctuary.</note> and like the holy ones; even so³ shall your seed and the children of your children be for glory, greatness, and holiness. He shall make them great throughout all the worlds. And they shall be princes, governors, and messengers for all the seed of the children of **Jacob**. They shall speak the word of the **Lord** in righteousness, and all His judgments they shall execute in righteousness. They shall declare His way to **Jacob** and His revelation⁵ to **Israel**. The blessing of the **Lord** shall be placed in their mouth, so that they may bless all the seed⁶ of the beloved <note>Jacob is often referred to as 'the beloved' in this context.</note>. As for you, your mother⁷ called you **Levi** <note>original: "ሌዊ" (Lewi). The name is traditionally associated with 'joining' or 'attachment' to God’s service.</note>, and truly she called you by your name; for the birth of the **Lord**; you shall be in the midst of all the children of **Jacob**. Let Him be your portion; and you and your children¹⁰ shall be ancient¹¹ and for all generations. Your grain shall be full, and your harvest¹² shall not fail for all ages. And all who hate you¹³ shall fall before your holiness¹⁴; and all your enemies shall be uprooted¹⁵ and perish. And those who bless you shall be blessed¹⁶, and all nations who curse you shall be cursed.

And to **Judah** also they said: May the **Lord** give you power and strength to tread down all those who hate you. You and one of your children shall be with the children of **Jacob**. Your name and the name of your children shall be¹⁷ as those who walk and travel through all the earth and its cities. Then¹⁸ shall the nations fear from before your face..."

<meta>This page continues the legal discourse following Reuben’s transgression with Bilhah. The text transitions from the specific narrative of the patriarchs into a broader delivery of divine law to Moses, establishing that sexual impropriety—specifically incest—is a capital offense that threatens the sanctity of the entire nation of Israel.</meta>

It is an impurity. <summary>This page outlines the strict divine laws against incest, using Reuben’s act as a legal precedent. ### The Plague of the Firstborn and the Passover

They ate the **Passover** <term>original: "ፋሲካ" (Pasika) — the ritual meal commemorating the Israelites' deliverance from Egypt</term> in **Egypt**, and all the powers of **Mastema** <note>The chief of the malevolent spirits, here acting under divine mandate to strike the Egyptians</note> were released in **Egypt**¹ to kill every firstborn in the land of **Egypt**, from the firstborn of **Pharaoh** to the firstborn of the female servant at the grinding mill² and even the firstborn of the animals. And this was the sign³ which the **Lord** gave to them: in every house where they applied⁴ on the doorframe the blood of a yearling lamb, the ones roaming to kill⁵ would not enter⁶ the house to strike it, so that all who were inside the house might be saved because the sign⁷ of the blood was on the doorframe.

And the powers of the **Lord**⁸ performed everything that He had intended. The **Lord** commanded them, and all the children of **Israel** were kept safe⁹. But He brought¹⁰ a plague upon the **Egyptians**, upon every soul among them, from animals to humans and even to the dogs. And there was a very great plague in **Egypt**, and there was no house in **Egypt**¹² that did not have a corpse within it, along with weeping and lamentation.

But all of **Israel** remained eating the meat of the **Passover**, and drinking, and rejoicing. They were praising, and thanking, and blessing the **Lord** their **God**, the **God** of their fathers. They were prepared to depart from the yoke of **Egypt** and from its bondage¹³.

And you also <note>The text addresses Moses directly</note>, remember this day all the days of your life¹⁴, once a year on its day¹⁵, according to the whole law. Do not let the day pass from its appointed time or from month to month, for it is an eternal statute which was engraved¹⁶ on the **Heavenly Tablets** <term>original: "ጽላት፡ሰማይ" (tsillate semay) — the celestial records where the eternal laws and fates of humanity are written</term> concerning the children of **Israel**, so that they should observe it every year in its season¹⁷, once a year, throughout all their generations.

And there is no limit of days <note>Meaning this is an eternal statute, not a temporary one</note>, for He has ordained it forever. There are twelve parts to the day, for it was given for light, and the third part is for the evening; this is what the **Lord** commanded, that you should perform it between the evenings⁶. And the slaughtering shall not take place during any time of light, except during the period of the evenings⁵. And they shall eat it in the time of the evening until the third part of the night⁷. And whatever remains of any of its meat after the third part of the night, they shall burn⁸ it with fire. And they shall not boil it in water, nor eat it raw⁹, but rather roasted¹⁰ with fire. And they shall cook it with fire, roasting its head along with its legs and its inner parts; they shall roast it with fire. And there shall be no breaking of any bone within it at all, for no bone¹¹ shall be crushed among the children of **Israel**. Therefore the **Lord** commanded the children of **Israel** to perform the **Passover** at its appointed time, and there shall be no breaking of any bone within it at all, because it is a feast day and an ordained day¹². ### The Laws of the Sabbaths and Jubilees

9 And after this law, the days of the **Sabbaths** <term>Sabbaths: weekly days of rest, but also used here for cycles of years</term> shall not be neglected in the desert of **Sinai**, which is between **Elim** <margin>variant: Elam</margin> and **Sinai**. I told you about the **Sabbaths** of the land on **Mount Sinai**, and the **Jubilee** years <term>Jubilee: a 49-year cycle in the biblical calendar</term> within the **Sabbaths** of years <margin>variant: year</margin>, we also spoke to you. 

But its specific year we did not tell you until you enter into the land of His command. Then the land itself shall keep its **Sabbaths** while they dwell upon it <margin>variant: and their dwelling</margin>. And they shall know the **Jubilee** year. For this reason, I have ordained for you the weeks of years and the **Jubilees**, from the days of **Adam** until this day, which is **4,979** years. 

9 And the third year <note>This refers to a specific point in the 49-year cycle</note> of the sixth week <margin>variant: for 40 years</margin> is still far off for knowing the commandments of the **Lord**, until they cross the boundaries of the land of **Canaan**, crossing the **Jordan** toward its western side. And the **Jubilees** shall pass until **Israel** is purified from all fornication, guilt, uncleanness, error, sin, and transgression. Then they shall dwell in...

<summary>This page outlines the requirement to celebrate Passover only at the central Sanctuary and introduces the complex chronological system of Sabbaths and Jubilees, dating the revelation to 4,979 years after Adam.</summary>
<keywords>Passover, Moses, Sinai, Jubilee, Sabbath of years, Adam, Jordan, Canaan, purification</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the conclusion of the regulations regarding the Jubilee years and transitions into the specific prohibitions and laws governing the weekly Sabbath. It concludes the Book of Jubilees (Chapter 50), reinforcing the sanctity of the day of rest for the people of Israel.</meta>

...all the years <note>original: "መያር" (mayar) — an archaic term in Ge'ez used specifically in the Book of Jubilees to denote 'years' or 'times'</note> that are faithful, and there is no longer a place for **Satan**, and there is no place for evil. The time was purified from that moment until all days.

And behold, the commandment of the **Sabbaths** <term>Sabbath: the seventh day of the week, dedicated to rest and worship</term> I have written for you, and all the judgments of its laws. Six days you shall work, and the seventh day is the **Sabbath** of the **Lord** your **God**; do not do any work on it, you, or your children, or your male servants, or your female servants, or all your cattle, or the resident stranger who is with you <margin>variant: the word/thing which is with you</margin>. And the person who does any work on it <margin>variant: on it (feminine)</margin> shall die. And every <margin>variant: all</margin> man who defiles <margin>variant: profanes</margin> this day, who lies with a woman, or who speaks of performing work on it, so that he might do <margin>variant: to do</margin> on it a journey <margin>variant: journeying</margin> and concerning everything that is bought or sold; and he who draws water on it <margin>variant: for it</margin> which he did not prepare for himself on the sixth day; and he who lifts anything to carry it, to take it <margin>variant: to take it</margin> out of his tent or out of his house, shall die. 

Do not do any work on the **Sabbath** day that you have not prepared for yourselves on the sixth day, for eating and for drinking, and for resting <note>original: "አውረድ" (awrad) — literally to 'bring down' or 'descend,' here meaning to settle down for rest</note>, and for keeping the **Sabbath** from all work on this day. And to bless the **Lord** your **God** who gave you a day of festival and a holy day, and a day of the holy kingdom <margin>variant: a day of the kingdom and a holy and sacred day</margin> for all **Israel**; this day in your days <margin>variant: this day</margin> for all time. For it is a great honor that the **Lord** has given to **Israel**, to eat and to drink and to be satisfied on this feast day, and to rest on this day from all work of the labor of the children of men, except for sin.

<summary>This page outlines the strict laws of the Sabbath as recorded in the Book of Jubilees, listing prohibited activities such as labor, travel, commerce, and even drawing water, while emphasizing the day's purpose for rest and communal joy.</summary>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page concludes the final chapter of the Book of Jubilees (Chapter 50). It finishes the list of specific prohibitions for the Sabbath day—ranging from travel to labor and warfare—and concludes with a formal colophon marking the end of the book.</meta>

[Sa]tan, and offer <insert>and he brought</insert> offerings and sacrifices before the **Lord** for the days and for the **Sabbaths**. This work alone shall be done on the **Sabbath** days <margin>variant: in the days of the house</margin> in the sanctuary of the **Lord** your **God**, so that He may make atonement for Israel with a perpetual offering day by day for a memorial, which is acceptable before the **Lord**.

And they shall be received <margin>variant: they shall be favored</margin> forever, day by day, according to what I have commanded you. And every person who performs work on it, or who goes on a journey, or who transacts business in the field <note>original: "ወፋር" (wafār) — refers to the open field or agricultural commerce</note>—whether in his house or in any other place—and he who lights a fire, or he who acts maliciously <note>original: "ይጸልእ" (yeṣall’e) — literally 'to hate' or 'to loathe,' but in this context of Sabbath laws, it often refers to placing a burden upon or riding an animal</note> toward any animal, or he who travels by ship on the sea; and every person who strikes or kills anything, or who slaughters an animal or a bird, or who catches either a beast or a bird or a fish; and he who fasts or makes war on the **Sabbath** day—the person who does any of these things on the **Sabbath** day shall die, so that the children of Israel may keep the **Sabbath** <margin>variant: the Sabbaths</margin> according to the commandment of the **Sabbaths** of the earth, as it was written in the **Heavenly Tablets** <term>Heavenly Tablets: a recurring concept in this text representing a divine, pre-existing record of law and history</term> <margin>variant: the Tablets</margin> which He gave into my hands so that I might write for you the **Law** <margin>variant: the Law of</margin> of the times and the seasons according to the division of its days.

->Here ends the **Book of the Division of Days** <note>original: "መጽሐፈ፡ኵፋሌ፡መዋዕል" (Maṣḥafa Kufālē Mawā’el) — the traditional Ge'ez title for the Book of Jubilees</note>. May the prayer of the author be worthy before the<-

## ADDITIONS.

Page 11 line 17: Instead of **ወይበለኒ:** <note>original: "and he said to me"</note>, Manuscript T shows **ወይበለኒ:**.
» 14 » 18 » **እንተ:ወፀአ:** <note>original: "which went out"</note>, Manuscript T shows **እንዘ:ይወፅእ:** <note>original: "while coming out"</note>.
» 21 » 5 » **ይምሐጾሙ:**, Manuscript A shows **ይምሐጸሙ:**; see page 160, note 6.
» 26 » 20: The words **ስላሴ:ህጹፋት:** <note>original: "selasē heṣufāt"</note> are to be translated as *double* <note>original Greek: "διπሎῦς" (diplous)</note>.
» 37 » 19: Instead of **ዘይትሐጐል:** <note>original: "that which perishes"</note>, Manuscript A shows **ዘኢይትሐጐል:** <note>original: "that which does not perish"</note>, but Manuscript E has deleted the negative "not" (**ኢ**).
» 43 » 23: **ወፈጡ:**, which Manuscript A provides, means *to roast with fire*.
» 84 » 18: **ወሰካሕካሕ:**, which is placed in the annotation, is preferable to the reading in the main text.
» 104 » 16, note 10: It should be noted that the Ethiopic Vocabularies provide the reading **ሐምየ:ከቦ:** and interpret it as **ሕቦት:** <note>likely meaning a swelling or tumor</note>.
» 107 » 3: **ኅብስተ:ሐሊብ:** <note>literally "milk-bread" or cheese</note> seems to be explained by the passage in 1 Samuel 17:18.
» 142 » 7: Instead of **ትትመሀስቢ:**, which Manuscript T provides, it should read **ትትመሀሰቢ:**.
» 143 » 1: Instead of **ወይበሎሙ:** <note>original: "and he said to them"</note>, which Manuscript A provides, it should read **ወይበልዎ:** <note>original: "and they said to him"</note>.
